› Message from fabrik:builder
  The statement pass now reads the `where` of slice 7, and every supported form
  is checked against SQLite. `make test`, `make lint` and `make check-browser`
  pass. The branch is `where-stmt`: 5 commits from `8a6dae5`, the head of
  `null-checks`, which is now merged as `5f5cb40` on main. Nothing is pushed.

  **What I built.** The recognizer, the typed form,
  `stmttest.Generator.Conditions` (the pass's tests set it to 4) and the hook
  `Generator.Where`. `stmttest.Sequence` sets neither. A dump of 63,000 texts
  and arguments from 30 seeds was byte for byte the same before and after, so
  the engine and driver tests draw the same calls. Until the engine runs slice
  7, `checkWhere` in `internal/engine/errors.go` refuses a select with WHERE as
  "WHERE, which the engine does not run yet". It runs in `engine.Check` and
  `prepare`, and `NewPlan` panics on such a select. The driver's `check` calls
  `engine.Check` again. The engine and `drivercheck` tests have the `checkText`
  wrappers again, because `stmttest.Others` has two WHERE texts the pass now
  supports.

  **Shape of a condition.**
  - `Select.Where []stmt.Condition` holds the conditions in text order, joined
  by AND, and is nil without WHERE.
  - Each `Condition` has `Column` (a `Name`), `Operator` and `Value` (a
  `stmt.Term`, as for an insert). For `OpIsNull` and `OpIsNotNull`, `Value` is
  the zero Term.
  - There are eight operators. `=`/`==` are one operator, and so are `!=`/`<>`:
  the tokenizer gives each pair one kind of token, and nothing s4ql shows prints
  the operator.
  - `Select.Parameters()` counts the `?` in the conditions.

  **Decisions of detail** (in `docs/todo.md`; the refinements are also in slice
  7 of the roadmap, status line untouched):
  - New names: `value on the left of a comparison`, `column compared with a
  column`, `column with the name of a table`, `OR` (offset is the first OR after
  the conditions before it), `IS DISTINCT FROM`, `IS with a value other than
  NULL`.
  - `isnull`, `notnull` and `not null` keep the name `NULL test`.
  - `WHERE deeper than SQLite's limit`: SQLite refuses an expression deeper than
  1000 while it parses. That allows at most 999 conditions, or 998 if one of
  the first two has a sign. I treat this like `too many SQL variables`;
  `TestCheck` checks both edges against SQLite.

  **Pinned numbers.**
  - `TestGenerate_mutants` moved from 77/305 to 54/284, and
  `TestGenerate_nearMisses` from 2,942 to 3,003. The generator draws other
  random numbers with `Conditions` on. Grouping the excused texts by message
  found no new kind and none with WHERE; 515 CREATE TABLE among the 2,000
  statements, against 502 before, explains the near misses.
  - The corpus's supported texts went from 13,454 to 14,692 (1,238 more SELECT,
  which I take to be the ones with WHERE).
  - The engine's and `drivercheck`'s numbers did not move. I changed four test
  texts:
    - `TestExec`, `TestTx` and one step of `TestSequences` now use ORDER BY for
  a text the pass refuses.
    - `TestPrepare`, `TestEngine_parameters`, `TestSequences` and a new
  `TestEngine_where` pin the engine's refusal.
  - `internal/sql/mutation` passes and no key moved.

  **For the engine builder** (all checked in the pass's tests, by `checkWhere`
  or by hand):
  - A comparison with NULL, as a literal or a nil argument, gives no rows.
  - SQLite resolves the select list first, then WHERE in text order. The first
  comparison of a missing column gives `no such column`.
  - A missing column inside IS NULL or IS NOT NULL does not stop it, because
  `resolveExprStep` walks that operand itself and ignores the abort. A later IS
  test of a missing column replaces the message, and the next comparison stops
  the walk without looking up its own column. So `a is null and b is null` gives
  `b`, and `a is null and b = 1` gives `a`.
  - `rowid`, `oid` and `_rowid_` are `no such column` in a WITHOUT ROWID table.
  - Rows always come in key order: SQLite searches the primary key for a
  condition on it and otherwise scans in key order.

  **Self-review.** The review found no bugs and five nits; I fixed four:
  - the doc comment of `Others`;
  - a condition's value no longer goes through the insert hook
  `Generator.Value`;
  - new `TestGenerator_Where`, since nothing ran the hook;
  - the row-spread check in `TestGenerate` only applies from 100 selects with
  WHERE.

  Left as in slice 6: `Prepare` parses a supported text twice until the engine
  runs WHERE. I also made the recognizer accept the ORs, so a reject before an
  OR is named for what holds it. Other checks:
  - Planted mutants (`<` read as `<=`, IS NOT NULL read as IS NULL, a dropped
  condition) each failed `checkWhere`.
  - `-count=20`, also under `-race`, passed.
  - Fuzzing found nothing: `FuzzLiteral` for 30 s, `FuzzGenerate` and
  `FuzzCheck` for 45 s each.
  - Coverage under `-short` is 99.4%.

  **Where a reviewer should look:** `conditions`, `condition`, `isCondition` and
  `leftColumn` in `/internal/sql/stmt/recognize.go`; `checkWhere` and its `no
  such column` rule in `/internal/sql/stmt/stmt_test.go`; and the slice 7
  decisions in `/docs/todo.md`. The diary is
  `/docs/diary/2026-11-20-where-stmt.md`, steps 1 and 2.

  **Follow-ups:** the engine and driver for slice 7, which remove `checkWhere`,
  the driver's `engine.Check` call and the wrappers; mutation testing and long
  fuzz runs of slice 7 in the pass; and the `roadmap` section of
  `site/index.html` after the merge.

  Created PR #80 (ctrl+o to expand)

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The WHERE of slice 7 runs end to end, through the engine and through
  `database/sql`. Everything is committed on branch `where-engine`: 7 commits on
  top of `origin/where-stmt` at `8d150cc`, not pushed. `make test`, `make lint`
  and `make check-browser` pass. The new tests pass with `-count=20`, also
  under `-race`.

  **What I built**
  - **Plan:** a select with WHERE gets a `Filter` (its `Condition`s in the order
  of the text) below the `Project`. `Format` prints `filter v1 >= ?1 and v2 IS
  NOT NULL`.
  - **Filter:** uses SQLite's three-valued logic. A comparison with NULL on
  either side is never true; `is null` and `is not null` are never unknown.
  - **Comparison (`compare`):** integers and reals by value, with 0.0 equal to
  -0.0; text and blobs by bytes, with the shorter first. Checked against SQLite
  at the edges of every type.
  - **Exact types:** a value of another type is refused as not supported. The
  messages are `s4ql: not supported: a string literal compared with the column
  t.a of type INT` and `argument 1, which is text, compared with the column
  ...`. NULL is never refused. NaN, infinities, NUL, invalid UTF-8 and a nil
  `[]byte` are refused before the instance, as for an insert.
  - **Removed:** `checkWhere`, the `checkText` wrappers, and the second parse in
  the driver's `check`. `Prepare` still refuses `or` and `order by` at once.

  **How the plan reads rows: it uses the key.**
  - The core gained `core.Bound`, `ReadTx.ScanRange` and `Tx.ScanRange`. `Scan`
  is now `ScanRange` without bounds, and the cache adds `where k >= ?1 and k <
  ?2` to its key-ordered query. `TestScanRange` compares every pair of bounds at
  every edge value of each type with a filtered full scan, in read and write
  transactions.
  - `keyAccess` picks, in this order:
    1. No rows, for a comparison with NULL or `key is null`.
    2. `get`, for the first `key = value`.
    3. `scanRange`, with the tightest bounds of `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` on the key.
    4. A full scan otherwise.
  - The filter still tests every condition on every row, so the access can never
  add a row. Whether it drops one is what the tests check.
  - `Options.scanAll` (set in tests through `Options.ScanAll`) reads every row.
  `TestEngine_where` runs every query on an engine that reads by the key, on one
  with `scanAll`, and on SQLite. `TestDifferential` and `FuzzDifferential_core`
  take `-scanall`.
  - `drivercheck` cannot run with `scanAll`, because the public options have no
  such knob.

  **Order of errors, confirmed in SQLite and pinned**
  1. Text and arguments, as before.
  2. `no such table`.
  3. `no such column` for the select list.
  4. `no such column` for the conditions, in the order of the text. A missing
  column under `is null` or `is not null` does not stop SQLite, a later such
  test replaces the name, and the next comparison ends the walk: `x is null and
  y is null` gives `y`, `x is null and y = 1` gives `x`. A name in double quotes
  gets SQLite's "should this be a string literal" message.
  5. `string or blob too big`, for an argument or a literal.
  6. Not supported, for the first condition with a value of another type, also
  inside a read-only transaction.

  A select leaves the count of changes.

  **Decisions of detail** (in `docs/todo.md`, with short refinements under slice
  7 in `docs/roadmap.md`): the plan shape, the messages, the order of errors,
  the key access and `scanAll`, and the new rules `stmttest.ConditionRule` and
  `stmttest.MissingColumn`. `drivercheck`'s `checkQuoted` now also covers a
  quoted name in a condition. Also recorded there is a known difference: a
  too-long blob literal in WHERE is NULL in SQLite, and a too-long string
  literal as the upper bound of the key gives no rows in SQLite when the search
  reaches no row. s4ql gives `string or blob too big` for both, as it does for
  an insert.

  **Pinned numbers that moved.** The generator draws as before; the only texts
  with WHERE are the two in `stmttest.Others`, which the engine used to refuse
  and now runs.
  - Engine `TestDifferential`: `excusedType` 339 to 350 (11 compare a value of
  another type).
  - `drivercheck`: `excusedPrepare` 1060 to 1063 (3 compare a missing column).
  - `drivercheck`: `excusedType` 161 to 169 (8 compare a value of another type).
  - `drivercheck`: `excusedQuoted` 45 to 48 (3 compare a quoted name that is not
  a column).

  The simulation's minimums held.

  **Review.** Two reviewers checked about 40,000 random queries each against
  SQLite and found no wrong row or error. I fixed:
  - The doc of `Bound`, which only described a lower bound.
  - Dead code in `scan.rows` and `keyAccess`.
  - The untested error path of `get` and the unpinned no-row access: `fake` now
  has `getFails` and `scanned`.
  - The long-literal difference above.
  - Stale comments.

  I left `checkCompared` duplicating `checkType`, which both reviewers flagged.
  Moving `checkType`'s body could invalidate equivalent-mutant entries on the
  other builder's mutation-testing branch; merging them is a small follow-up
  after that branch lands.

  **Left for the builder of the generated checks** (in `docs/todo.md`):
  - WHERE in `stmttest.Sequence`, with reach counts and minimum numbers.
  - A CI or Makefile run with `-scanall`, extended to `TestConn_differential`
  and the simulation.
  - The simulation with WHERE and a planted bug.
  - Mutation testing of slice 7: engine, `ScanRange`, driver.
  - Fuzz targets with WHERE.
  - A long literal on the key's upper bound must stay out of the generator or be
  checked against s4ql's rule.
  - `core.Bound` holds a single value, so a key of several columns would need it
  changed.

  **Where a reviewer should look**
  - `keyAccess`, `tighter`, `build.node` and `build.filter` in
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a7c913cdab83b320f/int
  ernal/engine/operator.go`
  - `conn.scan` and `table.checkBounds` in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.cla
  ude/worktrees/agent-a7c913cdab83b320f/internal/core/cache.go`
  - `checkCompared` in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-
  a7c913cdab83b320f/internal/engine/errors.go`
  - `TestEngine_where` in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/age
  nt-a7c913cdab83b320f/internal/engine/engine_test.go`
  - The decisions of slice 7 in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktre
  es/agent-a7c913cdab83b320f/docs/todo.md`

  The diary is `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a7c913cd
  ab83b320f/docs/diary/2026-11-20-where-engine.md`, in two steps.

⏺ Agent "Mutation testing slices 5 and 6" is waiting on its own background work 
  · 2h 7m 37s

› Message from general-purpose
  **Verdict: merge as is.** I found nothing that gives an application wrong rows
  or a wrong error. I reviewed `where-engine` at 6728627 (detached in my
  worktree), changed nothing on the branch, and the worktree is clean.

  **What I ran**
  - `go test -short ./internal/engine/ ./internal/core/ .` passes in all three
  packages (22s, 95s, 5s).
  - **Planted fault 1:** I made the upper bound always open in `keyAccess`, so
  the row at an inclusive bound is dropped. `TestEngine_where` failed at once on
  `k <= 0` (INT, INTEGER), `k <= 0.0`, `k <= ''` and `k <= x''`, and in the
  shapes subtest on `A <= 20`. `TestFilter` failed too. I restored the file.
  - **Throwaway randomized test:** 6,000 selects with 2 to 4 conditions, mostly
  on the key, over INT, REAL, TEXT and BLOB keys. Values were edges and values
  beside them, as literals, `?` arguments and `null`, plus `!=`, `is null` / `is
  not null`, and conditions on another column. Probes included `x''`,
  `x'0000'`, `x'80'`, `'ab'` against stored `'a'`, and `-0.0`. Each query ran by
  key, with `scanAll`, and on SQLite. 2,051 gave rows, 0 gave errors, and none
  differed. With fault 2 planted (lower bound always open) it failed. I deleted
  the test.

  **Findings by your points**
  1. **`keyAccess` and `tighter`:** correct.
     - A comparison with null on any column, and `key is null`, give no rows.
  That is right, because such a condition is never true.
     - `!=` and `is not null` on the key do not narrow the read.
     - The first `=` becomes a `get`. A second, different `=`, or bounds that
  exclude the value, are left to the filter.
     - `tighter` cannot drop a row whichever bound it picks: either one gives at
  least the rows that both bounds allow, and the filter re-tests every
  condition. The only risky parts are the mapping from operator to open/closed
  and the core's SQL, and the tests catch both.
     - The access is computed on bound values in `build.filter` on every run, so
  a prepared statement re-run with other arguments gets a fresh access.
  `conn_test.go` re-runs one prepared range query.
     - Real `-0.0`: `bind` turns `-0.0` into `0.0`, and `compare` uses
  `cmp.Compare`, for which `-0.0 == 0.0`. Infinity and NaN cannot reach a bound:
  the pass rejects `1e999`, and `checkArgument` rejects NaN and infinite
  arguments.
  2. **`conn.scan` and `checkBounds` in the core:**
     - The SQL is `>`, `>=`, `<`, `<=` with `?1`/`?2`, for one side or both. The
  cache table is STRICT with BINARY collation, so SQLite's comparison there
  matches `compare`.
     - `checkBounds` uses `checkKey`/`checkValue`, so the core also refuses an
  integer bound on a TEXT key, a bound on a key of two columns, NULL, and an
  over-long value (`TestScanRange` covers these).
     - `BindBlob` binds `x''` as the empty blob, not NULL.
     - A write transaction's scan runs on the same cache connection, so it sees
  rows it inserted and has not committed; `pinTx` checks this (`k > 1 and k < 5`
  after an insert of 3).
     - `TestScanRange` checks every pair of edge bounds, open and closed,
  against a model.
  3. **`compare` and the filter:** `strings.Compare` and `bytes.Compare` are
  memcmp with the shorter value first, which is BINARY. Three-valued logic is
  right: `!=` with null gives no rows, and `is null` on the key gives none. The
  edge test covers every operator by every edge on the key and on another
  column.
  4. **Error order and `checkCompared` vs `checkType`:**
     - The order is names (select list, then conditions by SQLite's walk), then
  length, then type, then the key access. So `b = null and a = ? and c = 'x'` is
  still refused as not supported, and that case is pinned.
     - `checkCompared` and `checkType` have identical logic and differ only in
  the message ("compared with" / "for"), so they agree. The duplication is a
  cleanup for later, and the builder left it on purpose because of the mutation
  testing running on that code.
  5. **The too-long literal (known difference):** s4ql gives `string or blob too
  big` only where SQLite gives no rows:
     - for a blob literal, which SQLite reads as NULL;
     - for a string literal as the upper bound of the key, when SQLite's search
  reaches no row.

     It does not matter. It needs a length limit below SQLite's limit on the
  length of SQL text, and only `core.Options.LengthLimit` sets one; the driver
  does not expose it, so only tests can lower it.
  6. **The tests:** `newWhereTrio.query` really runs each query three ways
  (SQLite, by key, `scanAll`) and requires equal answers. It catches an
  inclusive-bound fault, as shown above.

  **Minor, no action needed**
  - `-scanall` runs in no CI job. This is already in `docs/todo.md`; until then
  `TestEngine_where` is what checks by-key reads against full scans.
  - The `get` branch of `scan.rows` does not re-check `ctx.Err()` after its
  single read, but the context still goes to `get`.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The generated checks for slice 7 are built on `where-checks` (from
  `origin/where-engine` at 6728627, now at d120865, not pushed). The checks
  found no difference from SQLite or mattn, and none between the two ways of
  reading rows. `make test`, `make lint` and `make check-browser` pass.

  **What I built**
  - **Generator:** `stmttest.Sequence` writes WHERE through the hook
  `Generator.Where` (`Sequence.where`, `conditionValue`, `neighbour`, `edgeOf`,
  `store`). One select in two has a WHERE, one in five of those is a key range,
  and it can have up to eight conditions. Values mostly come from what the
  inserts gave each column (kept without a random draw, so inserts draw as
  before), their neighbours, and the edges of each type. NULL comes as a literal
  and as a nil argument. Every fault in your list is reached.
  - **Shared rule:** `stmttest.WhereCases` and `KeyAccess` in `rule.go` are used
  by both the engine test and drivercheck.
  - **Key-access cross-check:** `reachWhere` compares `KeyAccess` with the new
  `engine.AccessOf` (in export_test.go). This catches a plan that reads more
  rows than the rule says, which comparing rows cannot see. A mutant that
  dropped the `get` of `keyAccess` failed at once.
  - **Fuzz:** new `FuzzWhere` and `FuzzWhere_core`. A minute each gave 222,955
  and 11,619 executions and found nothing.

  **Where both ways are compared**
  - `pair.step` runs every call with a select with WHERE a second time the other
  way (`Engine.SetScanAll` / `ScansAll`, export_test.go only). It requires the
  same answer, message and count of changes. This covers TestDifferential,
  TestConn_differential (in transactions too), FuzzDifferential(_core), FuzzSQL,
  FuzzTypes and FuzzWhere, with or without `-scanall`.
  - In the simulation, every second engine of a node reads every row. The hammer
  workload opens once, so it reads by the key only.
  - drivercheck reads by the key only. The driver has no public knob for it.

  **Planted bug `openBound`**
  - `bugs.reader` wraps the reader of a query, and its `scanRange` reads a
  closed bound as open. I did not change the signature of `buildQuery`, so the
  other builder's `operator_internal_test.go` is untouched.
  - It is found by 20 of 200 seeds, all by its own violation ", and in SQLite",
  and by none without the bug.
  - The other bugs over 200 seeds, old → new (own violation in parentheses):
  skipUnique 163(156)→165(158), ackUnknown 45→47, checkOutside 65→61, keepOpen
  198→199, keepRead 98(54)→80(37), annotateNext 160→156, keepFailed 188→180,
  readNewest 185(164)→178(154), rollbackKeeps 104→109, convertReal 5(5)→16(12),
  listOrder 112(44)→118(39), zeroLeftOut 52(44)→39(36).

  **Pinned numbers**
  Every count moves because the sequence now draws different random numbers. The
  specific reasons are in comments beside the pins.
  - **TestDifferential:**
    - acceptedStartedTable 18→19 and excusedArguments 703→718.
    - excusedArgument 220→248: rejected arguments around the WHERE parameters.
    - excusedType 350→478: 112 selects compare a value of another type than the
  column.
    - excusedLong 8→4.
    - New `excusedLongCondition` pinned at 0. A long literal in a condition is
  checked against s4ql's rule: the engine must give `string or blob too big`
  where SQLite gives no error. The default run reaches 2 such selects, and
  SQLite refuses both too. A mutant that skipped this length check failed at
  seed 266.
  - **TestGenerated:**
    - excusedPrepare 1063→1178 and excusedQuoted 48→69: missing columns, some in
  double quotes.
    - excusedType 169→231.
    - excusedArgument 200→260.
    - excusedRowsOpen 46→24: selects with WHERE fail more often, so fewer rows
  are left open in transactions.
    - Others: excusedStartedTable 10→11, excusedArguments 426→411, excusedLevel
  74→69, excusedTxOpen 29→24, excusedOutside 53→58.
  - **New least numbers:**
    - Both differential tests have one for each operator on the key and on
  another column, each access path, a select giving none, some or all rows of a
  table with rows, each fault, and each kind of value. They are a third of the
  run.
    - TestConn_differential requires 146 selects read both ways, 32 of them in a
  read-only transaction.
    - TestSimulation requires a quarter of what 1,000 seeds give for selects
  with WHERE compared with SQLite.

  **Run times:** these are noisy, because other sessions kept the machine at
  load average 22.
  - `internal/engine` went from 42.9 s to 46.0 s. TestSimulation_plantedBugs
  went from 5.7 s to 9.8 s, mostly `openBound`, which needs about 20 seeds for
  its two finds.
  - `internal/sql/stmt` went from 51.1 s to 45.8 s; its code did not change, so
  this is noise.
  - drivercheck went from 11.7 s to 10.1 s.
  - All new tests passed with `-count=20`, also with `-race`.

  **Review:** the `code-review` skill found one real fault, now fixed: under
  `-scanall` the second run was off, so the new "read both ways" least numbers
  failed. It also found four nits, all fixed. A writer sub-agent updated

  I checked and corrected its diff.

  Files changed: `README.md`, `site/index.html` (rows "Works now", "Checked",
  "Not built", "Next", and slice 7 marked Built), `docs/roadmap.md` (slice 7's
  status line; the seed counts dropped from slices 4, 5, 6 and from `nullZero`),
  and `AGENTS.md` (one sentence plus the quote, and one matching clause).

  New "Status" of the README, word for word (after "As of 2026-11-20:"):
  - **Works now,** through `database/sql`, with the package `maragu.dev/s4ql` as
  the driver: `create table` and `drop table` of a strict table with one or
  more columns, exactly one of them the primary key. / The column types `int`,
  `integer`, `real`, `text`, and `blob`. A value must have the exact type of its
  column. / Columns that are `not null`, and columns that take null. / `insert`
  of one row, with literals and arguments, with or without a list of columns. /
  `select` of named columns or `*`, with a `where` of comparisons and of `is
  null` and `is not null`, joined by `and`. / Arguments for a plain `?`,
  directly and through prepared statements. / Transactions, also read-only ones.
  / Storage: a database is a log of commits in S3, with a local SQLite cache.
  One instance at a time holds a lease on it. Commits, recovery after a crash,
  and replay of the log work.
  - **How it is checked:** against SQLite with generated statements, in the
  engine and through `database/sql`, in a deterministic simulation with planted
  bugs, and with mutation testing and fuzzing. The storage is also tested
  against a real S3-compatible bucket in CI.
  - **Not built:** `update` and `delete`, `or` and other expressions in a
  `where`, `order by` and `limit`, more than one row per `insert`, indexes,
  defaults, and a statement while rows of the same transaction are open.
  - **Next:** slice 8 of the roadmap (`update` and `delete`), which is agreed
  and not built yet. After it comes the rest of what the first application needs
  (the roadmap's [milestones](docs/roadmap.md#milestones)).
  Then: "The slices, and how each is checked, are in
  [docs/roadmap.md](docs/roadmap.md). What is open in detail is in
  [docs/todo.md](docs/todo.md)." (In the README the eight "Works now" items are
  sub-bullets; I joined them with "/" only to fit this report.)

  Three things to check:
  - **Dropped from the old status:** the parser corpus numbers, the cache that
  refuses another database, and the S3 client and Tigris line on the site.
  - **Mutation testing overstates a little:** the "How it is checked" sentence
  names mutation testing as you asked, but it has covered slices 2 to 4 only.
  Slices 5 to 7 are still to come.
  - **Slice 7's text against the code:** I found no contradiction. One sentence
  is not quite true of SQLite: "After these comes `string or blob too big` for a
  value of a condition that is too long". s4ql always gives that error. SQLite
  gives no rows for a long blob literal, and none for a long string literal used
  as the upper bound of the key when the search reaches no row. `docs/todo.md`
  already records this as a known difference.

✻ Crunched for 14s · done Friday 12:49 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  No order after slice 8 is proposed anywhere. roadmap.md, Milestones: "Slices 5
  to 8 lead to it. After slice 8 comes the rest of what glue needs, which is
  not agreed as slices yet." The glue note (docs/research/glue-on-s4ql.md)
  section 5 ranks gaps "from needed nearly everywhere to needed in one place"
  but gives no sequence. docs/todo.md names no later slice for glue; only line
  337 is related: replay applies results only, so triggers/cascades must not run
  at replay.

  MISSING AFTER SLICE 8. [A] = a change in glue/goqite/migrate/scs could remove
  it. Section numbers refer to the glue note.
  1. A literal or `?` on the left of a comparison (`? >= timeout`, `received <
  ?`).
     (a) goqite ReceiveTx; section 2 row "Comparisons >= and <", gap 2. Slice 7
  rejects it.
     (b) Depends on nothing. Could be [A] by writing the column on the left.
  2. `+` arithmetic in `set`.
     (a) goqite ReceiveTx update; section 2 "Update with arithmetic", gap 6.
  Slice 8 rejects any expression in set.
     (b) Needs an expression evaluator, which items 5, 6 and 11 reuse.
  3. `default <literal>`.
     (a) goqite schema, `default 0` twice; gap 5. Slice 6 rejects default.
     (b) Needs commit format to carry a default, plus insert to apply it.
  Depends on nothing else. Expression defaults are [A] (goqite makes ids and
  times in Go).
  4. `create table if not exists`.
     (a) migrate, for the migrations table; gap 5.
     (b) Depends on nothing.
  5. `order by` (with `desc`) and `limit` on select.
     (a) goqite's scalar subquery `order by priority desc, created limit 1`; gap
  8.
     (b) Depends on nothing for plain select.
  6. A scalar subquery in `where` (`id = (select ... limit 1)`) inside `update`.
     (a) goqite ReceiveTx; gap 8.
     (b) Depends on slice 8 and items 1, 2 and 5.
  7. `create index`, with `desc` and several columns.
     (a) goqite index on (queue, priority desc, created) and sessions(expiry);
  gap 5.
     (b) Depends on item 5 for ordering semantics. Needs a commit-format change;
  the planner can use it later.
  8. Several statements in one Exec.
     (a) every migration file and 2 fixtures; gap 5.
     (b) [A] only if migrate and the fixture loader split statements. The
  trigger's `begin ... end;` makes splitting on `;` unsafe, so s4ql splitting is
  safer. Depends on nothing, but a trigger in the text depends on item 9.
  9. Trigger (`after update on goqite`).
     (a) goqite schema; gap 10. [A]: goqite could set `updated` in its 2
  updates.
     (b) Depends on item 2 and on todo.md line 337.
  10. `returning` (insert ... returning id; update ... returning 2 columns).
      (a) goqite Send and ReceiveTx; gap 7. [A] if goqite makes the id in Go and
  selects before it updates.
      (b) The update form depends on slice 8.
  11. `select` without `from`, aliases, functions and subqueries: `select 1`,
  `as`, `count(*)`, `exists`, `strftime`, `julianday`,
  `lower`/`hex`/`randomblob`, `||`.
      (a) Ping in sql/helper.go; migrate.go for `exists`; gaps 6, 8, 9. The
  production uses are only 1 `select 1` and 1 `exists`, both [A] (use
  PingContext).
      (b) Depends on the expression evaluator.
  12. Test-only: `count(*)` x5, `in (?, ?)`, `union` in `from`, `sqlite_master`,
  `drop table`. All [A] or test code.
  13. `REPLACE INTO` and `$1` (scs sessions) are [A]: glue writes its own
  scs.Store.
  Not SQL and not a slice: glue must accept a `*sql.DB` or a driver name (gap
  11, [A], required).

  PROPOSED ORDER (smallest steps, each cheap to check against SQLite, earlier
  ones unblock later ones):
  - 9: `if not exists` (item 4). The smallest step; only the pass and DDL
  change.
  - 10: `default <literal>` (item 3). The commit format and the cache version
  change.
  - 11: a literal or `?` on the left of a comparison (item 1). A pure `where`
  change on top of slice 7.
  - 12: `order by` and `limit` on select, with `desc` (item 5). Easy to compare
  with SQLite.
  - 13: the first expression evaluator, `set col = col + <literal or ?>` (item
  2). The core of what follows.
  - 14: a scalar subquery with `order by ... limit 1` in `where` (item 6), after
  11 to 13.
  - 15: `create index` (item 7), with its commit-format change.
  - 16: several statements per Exec (item 8), once it is decided whether s4ql or
  glue splits.
  - Optional, all [A]: `returning` (10), the trigger (9), functions and `select`
  without `from` (11).

  Caveat: this is my reading, not an agreed plan. The exact shape of goqite's
  update and subquery is taken from the glue note's table, not from reading
  goqite.

⏺ Agent "How the cache stores data" finished · 34s

› Message from general-purpose
  Read from internal/core/cache.go, tx.go and README.md in the checked-out main.
  Items marked (inferred) are not read directly.

  1. Tables in the cache file besides the user's tables
  - There is exactly one: `s4ql_cache`. Its text is:
    create table main.s4ql_cache (id integer primary key check (id = 1), version
  integer not null, head text not null, database text not null) strict
  - It always has one row. `version` is the layout version (`cacheVersion` = 2).
  `head` is the position, meaning the name of the last commit object applied
  (64 hex digits, or `format.EmptyLog` for a new cache). `database` is
  `Options.Identity`. Open refuses a cache with another identity
  (`ErrCacheDatabase`).
  - Names starting with `s4ql_` or `sqlite_` are reserved. User tables cannot
  use them.
  - The temp tables of item 3 live only in `temp`, never in the file.

  2. A user table
  - Yes, it is a real SQLite table with the same name, in `main`.
  `table.createSQL` generates it. The spelling of the name is kept, and names
  are compared by SQLite's rules.
  - It is `strict, without rowid`, with the primary key as a table constraint at
  the end. Key columns get no `not null`, because a primary-key column of a
  without-rowid table never takes NULL. A nullable column gets `default null`.
  Every other non-key column gets `not null`. Types are those of strict tables
  (not ANY).
  - For `t (a int primary key, b text not null, c real)` the output is, by my
  reading of the generator (not run):
    create table main."t" (a integer, b text not null, c real default null,
  primary key ("a")) strict, without rowid
  - The exact type spelling (`integer` vs `int`) comes from `typeSQL`, which I
  did not open. The identifiers are quoted by `quote()`.
  - `conn.load` reads the table back with `pragma_table_info`. It tells a
  nullable column by the default text `null`. Any other default, a default on a
  key column, or `not null` together with `default null` is refused with
  `ErrCacheVersion`.

  3. How write data gets in
  - A write transaction writes directly into the cache, in an open SQLite write
  transaction on the single write connection. Each statement runs inside the
  savepoint `s4ql_statement`.
  - At commit, the commit object is built from the changed rows and uploaded to
  S3 before the manifest lock is taken. The new position is written into the
  open SQLite transaction before the manifest swap, so that after the swap the
  cache only has to run `commit`.
  - If the swap succeeds and the cache `commit` fails, `Commit` still returns
  nil and the instance stops. Recovery then replays the missing commit object
  from the log into the cache.
  - Replay (`replay.go`) is the path that applies commits from the log. It is
  used for catch-up and after "outcome unknown". Replay is strict: a commit
  object that does not fit stops the instance with `ErrLogBroken`.
  - `s4ql_touched_N` and `s4ql_existed_N` are temp tables, created on the first
  write to a table that existed when the transaction began. `N` is `tx.temps`.
    - `s4ql_touched_N` holds the primary key of every row the transaction
  upserted or deleted. A key goes in with `insert or ignore ... returning 1`
  after the write, so the first record of a key stays.
    - `s4ql_existed_N` holds the keys of those rows that already existed at the
  start. A key goes in only when the touched insert took it and the row existed.
    - Both are `strict, without rowid` with columns `k0..kn` of the key types.
  Commit joins them with the real table to write the commit object (net effect
  per row: insert, update or delete).
    - A table created in the same transaction gets no temp tables, because all
  its rows are new. They are dropped by `dropTouched`.
    - They live in the cache, not in memory, so a transaction can change any
  number of rows.

  4. Missing, stale or another version
  - Missing or empty file: the write connection creates it with `OpenVFS`.
  `setUp` makes `s4ql_cache` inside `begin immediate`, re-checking so that of
  two instances only one creates it. Catch-up then replays the whole log from
  `EmptyLog`.
  - Stale (behind the manifest head): catch-up walks the log back from the head
  to the cache's position and applies the commits oldest first. A power loss
  under `synchronous=normal` can leave the cache missing its last commits. It is
  never corrupt, and catch-up covers the gap.
  - Other identity: `ErrCacheDatabase`.
  - A file with tables but no `s4ql_cache`: `ErrCacheVersion`.
  - Version other than 1 or 2, or an unparsable position: `ErrCacheVersion`. The
  message says to open with a new cache or delete the file together with `-wal`
  and `-shm`.
  - Version 1: `conn.upgrade` raises it to 2 in place. It first checks that no
  non-key column holds NULL (`checkNoNull`).
  - This conflicts with CLAUDE.md, which says (on 2026-11-20, "Yes, remove it")
  that an in-place upgrade should be removed. On main the upgrade is still in
  `cache.go`, `record.check` accepts version 1, and the README documents it.

  5. SQLite settings
  - `pragma busy_timeout = Options.BusyTimeout` is set on the write connection.
  Busy after the timeout is returned as `ErrBusy` and logged as a warning.
  - `pragma journal_mode = wal` is required. If the mode is not wal, setUp
  fails. WAL lets readers run alongside the single writer, and the first
  connection's setUp recovers the WAL after a crash.
  - `pragma synchronous = normal`, or `off` when the test option `syncOff` is
  set. The reason is in the `setUp` doc comment. The cache may lose its last
  commits after a power loss but is never corrupt, and a commit is acknowledged
  from S3, so the extra sync per commit of `full` buys nothing.
  - Readers open the existing file only (`OpenExistingVFS`) with `pragma
  query_only = on`.
  - `max length` is SQLite's default of 1e9 bytes. The core checks rows against
  this limit.
  - There are no triggers and no foreign keys (stated in a comment at
  cache.go:1003).

⏺ Here is how the cache stores data, read from the code on main:

  - Your tables: each is a real SQLite table of the same name, declared strict, 
    without rowid, with the primary key as a table constraint. A nullable column
    carries default null, and every other non-key column carries not null.
  - Bookkeeping: one extra table, s4ql_cache, with a single row: the layout
    version (2), the name of the last commit applied from the log, and the
    database's identity. Names starting with s4ql_ or sqlite_ are reserved.
  - Writes: a transaction writes straight into the cache inside an open SQLite
    transaction. At commit, the changed rows go to S3 as a commit object first,
    then the manifest is swapped, and only then does the cache run its own
    commit.
  - Tracking changes: two temp tables per touched table record which keys the
    transaction changed and which of those existed before. Commit joins them
    with the real table to get the net insert, update, or delete per row, so a
    transaction can change any number of rows without holding them in memory.
  - Missing or stale cache: it is rebuilt or caught up by replaying commits from
    the log. A cache with another identity or version is refused.
  - Settings: WAL mode, so readers run beside the single writer, and synchronous
    = normal. A power loss can cost the cache its last commits but not corrupt
    it, and catch-up refills them from S3.

  The in-place upgrade from version 1 is still on main; the builder removing it
  is running.

  Still open from before: is create table if not exists fine as slice 9?

✻ Cooked for 1s · done Friday 12:56 PM

⏺ Agent "Add slices 11, 12 to roadmap" finished · 54s

❯ Hmm, although: I also like randomness. E.g. Go's map iteration, so no one     
  relies on it. Maybe even better. E.g. if no order by, then new random order   
  every time (unless it conflicts with SQLite)?                                 

⏺ I think you're right, and for a reason beyond Go's: a promised tie order binds
  the planner forever. Once indexes come (glue needs them), a query's natural
  order depends on which index is used, and a promise of primary key order would
  force an extra sort on every such query. Varying the order promises nothing
  and keeps that freedom.

  It doesn't conflict with SQLite: any order of unordered or tied rows is a
  result SQLite could give too. It also doesn't touch the log, since commits
  record resulting rows, not queries.

  One limit I'd set: no full shuffle, because that means holding the whole
  result in memory before the first row comes out. A cheap form instead: each
  query flips a coin and runs unordered scans and ties forward or backward by
  key. That keeps streaming, and breaks any reliance within a few runs. Tests
  and the simulation give the engine a seed, so a failure can be replayed.

  Revised wording for the spec:

  ▎ Where SQLite leaves the order of rows unspecified, s4ql varies it from one 
  ▎ query to the next, so that nothing can come to depend on it. This holds for 
  ▎ a select without order by and for rows that tie under order by.

  Agree to this instead of the deterministic rule?

  before this one is the one about Go's map iteration. Could you send it again?

✻ Cooked for 2s · done Friday 12:59 PM

⏺ Agent "Spec rule on row order" finished · 1m 13s

❯ Maybe we should tackle indexes or triggers first? The large building blocks   
  before the smaller ones.                                                      

⏺ Agreed on the principle: the large blocks shape the commit format and the
  engine's structure, so their design risk is better met early, while the small
  slices fit in anywhere.

  Of the two, I'd take indexes first, and not triggers yet:

  - Indexes depend on nothing we lack. They touch the commit format (a table's
    schema must carry them), the cache, and how the engine picks its way to
    rows, which today knows only the primary key. Everything in where and order 
    by builds on that choice.
  - Triggers need the pieces we haven't got: goqite's trigger body is an update
    with a function call (strftime) and a reference to the old row. They also
    need a rule for replay. And glue could avoid the trigger altogether by
    setting the column in its two updates.

  So my proposal: indexes become the next thing built after slice 8, ahead of
  slices 9 to 12, which stay agreed and follow. I'd then bring you the design
  decisions for indexes one at a time, starting with scope.

  Agree to that order?

✻ Churned for 2s · done Friday 1:01 PM

❯ Sure                                                                          

⏺ First decision on indexes, scope. My proposal:

  - In: create index i on t (a, b desc) and drop index i. One or more columns,
    each asc or desc. That covers both indexes glue has: goqite's on (queue, 
    priority desc, created) and the sessions table's on (expiry).
  - In: the engine uses an index to find rows for a where, as it uses the
    primary key today. An index that only exists would prove nothing.
  - Out, as a later slice of its own: unique indexes. They are a constraint more
    than a way to find rows: they add errors to insert and update, and glue's
    schemas don't seem to need one.
  - Out: partial indexes (where), indexes on expressions, and collate.

  Agree to that scope?

✻ Cooked for 11s · done Friday 1:02 PM

❯ Yes.                                                                          

⏺ Second decision, where an index lives. My proposal:

  - In the log: only the definition. create index writes a commit that says
    "table t has index i on (a, b desc)", and drop index one that removes it. No
    index entries go to S3, since they can always be derived from the rows.
  - In the cache: a real SQLite index on the table, which SQLite keeps up to
    date on every write. Each instance builds it when it replays the commit that
    defines it.
  - In the engine: s4ql decides which index a query uses, and the cache's SQLite
    then runs the range scan on exactly that index. So the choice stays ours
    and stays testable, and the search structure is SQLite's, which is well
    tested.

  The alternative, s4ql keeping its own index tables, is more code and more ways
  to be wrong, with nothing gained now.

  The cost: an instance that catches up on a create index for a large table
  spends local time building it. Commit objects stay small.

  Agree to that?

✻ Sautéed for 11s · done Friday 1:04 PM

❯ Yeah, sounds good. Do we support multiple index types?                        

⏺ No, one type. SQLite's ordinary indexes are all B-trees; there is no hash
  index and no using clause as in Postgres. What varies is what an index covers
  (unique, partial, on an expression), and those we just left out. Full-text and
  spatial search exist in SQLite as separate table modules, not as index types,
  and would be features of their own if we ever want them.

  Third decision, how the engine picks an index. My proposal is fixed rules and
  no statistics:

  - A where that pins the whole primary key uses the primary key.
  - Otherwise the engine takes the index whose leading columns the where pins
    with = for the longest stretch, plus a range on the next column if there is
    one.
  - A tie goes to the primary key first, then to the index created first.
  - No index fits: the scan of today.

  Rules like these make the choice predictable and easy to check: the tests run
  every query through every index that could serve it and require the same rows
  each time. Statistics (SQLite's analyze) could come later without changing any
  format.

  Agree to that?

✻ Sautéed for 11s · done Friday 1:05 PM

❯ See this is where my knowledge of indexes stops. I don't know what's a good   
  idea here. Should we subagent-research how other storage engines do this?     
  Including SQLite.                                                             

⏺ Agent(Research index selection) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes, good call; a research agent is on it now. It reads how SQLite,
  PostgreSQL, MySQL, CockroachDB (which started with fixed rules and later moved
  to costs), Oracle's old rule-based optimizer, MongoDB, and DynamoDB pick an
  index, from their docs and source. It then answers for s4ql: fixed rules or
  let SQLite pick, which rules, how order by ... limit enters (that is what
  goqite's index is really for), and what binds us now versus what we can change
  later. The result lands as docs/research/index-selection.md, and I'll bring
  you the recommendation as one decision.

✻ Churned for 36s · done Friday 1:07 PM

❯ I'm also tempted to just let SQLite decide, but we'll see.                    

⏺ It's a real option, and the research is asked to weigh it. One thing to keep
  in mind for when it reports: SQLite can only pick an index if it sees the
  whole query, so "let SQLite decide" means the cache's SQLite also evaluates
  the where and the order by, where today s4ql's own engine does that and asks
  the cache only for rows by key. That shifts work from our engine to SQLite's,
  and it weakens the check against SQLite, since we'd partly be comparing SQLite
  with itself. Whether that trade is worth it is the question the note should
  answer.

✻ Crunched for 25s · done Friday 1:09 PM

❯ Ah, right. Probably the wrong approach then.                                  

⏺ Probably, yes. It would quietly turn s4ql's engine into a translator for
  SQLite's, and the checks against SQLite are worth the most when s4ql does the
  work itself. I'll still let the research make the case either way, since it
  may turn up a middle path, such as fixed rules of ours that follow the order
  SQLite's planner would choose in the plain cases.

  **What SQLite does in the cases you listed:**
  - Twice in SET: the last value wins.
  - Missing columns, in this order: `no such table`, then SET columns in text
  order as `no such column: <value>` (no single-quote hint even for quoted
  names), then WHERE by slice 7's rule.
  - `main.t` works; `temp.t` gives `no such table: temp.t`.
  - `update t set a = 1 where` gives `incomplete input`; with `;` it is `near
  ";": syntax error`.
  - Trailing semicolons and comments are fine.
  - Keywords as names: the same names as in INSERT; in WHERE, the 84 of SELECT.
  - `rowid` in SET: `no such column: rowid` in a WITHOUT ROWID table.

✻ Baked for 17s · done Friday 1:12 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  I wrote `docs/research/index-selection.md` and committed it alone on `main` as
  `3771439` ("Add research on how engines pick an index"), then pushed it.

  **Recommendation:** s4ql's engine should pick the index by fixed rules, and
  the cache's SQLite should run the scan on that index with `indexed by`. Do not
  let the cache's SQLite pick.
  - Without statistics, SQLite's choice is fixed guesses too: a table of 1M
  rows, 10 rows per `=`, a range cuts the rows to 1/4, and both bounds to 1/64
  (`sqlite3DefaultRowEst`, `whereRangeScanEst`). Our build has no stat4, so
  bound values never change the plan.
  - Those guesses are hidden from our tests. The plan is only stable within one
  SQLite version, and when two indexes cost the same, the newest one wins (I ran
  this).
  - Letting SQLite pick also goes against the spec's rule that SQLite does not
  run the application's SQL.
  - Rules make the plan a function of the statement and the schema in the log.
  It is the same on every instance and can be checked by the simulation and by
  mutation testing.

  **Findings that bear most on your rules:**
  1. **Measured.** I made goqite `strict, without rowid`, filled it with 100k
  rows, and added a second index `(queue, timeout)`. SQLite then picks that
  index and sorts, both before and after `analyze`. When all rows of the queue
  are ready, that takes 19.4 ms against 0.048 ms for goqite's ordering index.
  When none are ready, the ordering index is the slow one: 20.8 ms against 0.062
  ms. Each index has a bad case of about 400 times. Your rules, as written,
  choose like SQLite here, so `order by` with `limit` needs a rule of its own.
  PostgreSQL ("abort-early plan disaster") and MySQL (the
  `prefer_ordering_index` switch since 8.0.21) document this same failure.
  2. **Supports your rules.** Rule-based choice by structure is what
  CockroachDB's first planner did, what Oracle's RBO did (an `=` outranks a
  range, which outranks the order), and what FoundationDB's Record Layer still
  does (fixed scores, "safer without knowing any cardinalities"). TiDB still
  prunes by dominance before it looks at costs. CockroachDB moved to costs
  mainly for joins and crossover points. Engines with statistics later added
  ways to hold plans still: Spanner's `FORCE_INDEX` and optimizer version
  pinning, Oracle's SQL plan management, InnoDB's persistent statistics.
  3. **Additions your rules need.**
     - Every index on a `without rowid` table ends with the primary key columns,
  and SQLite uses them as bounds. For example, `a=? AND k>?` runs on an index
  on `(a)`.
     - `not indexed` is silently ignored on `without rowid` tables, against the
  docs. The code checks it only for tables with a rowid. To force the table's
  own b-tree, name `sqlite_autoindex_<table>_1` instead.
     - `indexed by` seldom fails. Given an index that does not fit, it scans the
  whole index without an error, so tests must check with `explain query plan`
  that SQLite used the bounds s4ql asked for.

  **Proposed rules:**
  - The key that counts is the index's columns plus the primary key columns
  after them. `is null` counts as a pin, like `=`.
  - The most leading columns pinned by `=` wins.
  - With the same `=` prefix and a `limit`, an index that gives the order beats
  a range. Without a `limit`, the range wins.
  - With no prefix, an index that gives the order beats a full scan.
  - Ties go to the primary key, then a covering index, then the index created
  first.

  **Decisions for Markus:**
  1. Does s4ql pick the index by fixed rules, with no statistics and no
  `analyze` in the cache? I recommend yes.
  2. With the same `=` prefix and a `limit`, does an index that gives the order
  beat a range? I recommend yes. Its bad case is a queue with nothing ready, and
  that read is bounded by the rows of the queue.
  3. Are `indexed by`, `not indexed`, and `explain query plan` rejected in
  application SQL for now? I recommend rejecting them. The chosen access would
  show as an attribute of the statement's telemetry event instead. Supporting
  `explain query plan` needs a decision of its own, because its output would
  differ from SQLite's wherever the choices differ.
  4. May the rules change in later releases without a format change, since
  results cannot show the choice? I recommend yes, with each change noted in the
  release because it changes speed.

  **What binds now:** only the index definition in the log (name, table, ordered
  columns, a direction per column) and the errors of `create index` and `drop
  index`. The rules, the SQL sent to the cache, and the cache's layout can
  change freely.

  **How to verify it:**
  - Run every generated query through every candidate path, the full scan, the
  primary key range and each index forward and backward, and require SQLite's
  rows from each.
  - Check the chosen path against a small model of the rules.
  - Check the cache's statement with `explain query plan`, and compare the rows
  the cache gives with the rows that match.
  - Plant bugs for the simulation to find: a bound turned around on a `desc`
  column, an index key without its primary key suffix.
  - Run `pragma integrity_check` on the cache after replay and crashes.
  - Keep golden plans for pinned queries, goqite's receive among them, so a rule
  change shows as a diff.

  Caveat on sources: the quotes from web pages came through a fetch tool that
  summarises pages, and I did not compare them with the raw HTML. The note says
  so. The SQLite claims are from the vendored source or from runs of the
  vendored shell.

⏺ The research is in (docs/research/index-selection.md, 3771439). Its
  recommendation is fixed rules of our own, and not letting SQLite pick:

  - SQLite without statistics is fixed guesses too. It assumes a table of a
    million rows, ten rows per =, and that a range keeps a quarter. So "let
    SQLite decide" buys guesses we can't see or test, which may change with each
    SQLite version.
  - The spec already says SQLite does not run the application's SQL. Letting it
    pick would go against that.
  - Others did the same. CockroachDB's first planner, Oracle's old optimizer,
    and FoundationDB's Record Layer all pick by structure. Engines with
    statistics later added ways to hold plans still, because plans that flip are
    a known operational pain.
  - With rules, the choice depends only on the statement and the schema. It is
    the same on every instance and can be checked by simulation and mutation
    testing.

  One finding changes my proposed rules. The agent measured goqite's receive
  query on 100,000 rows with a second index present: SQLite and my rules both
  pick the wrong index when all messages are ready, about 400 times slower (19
  ms against 0.05 ms). So order by with limit needs a rule of its own: an index
  that already delivers the order beats one that only narrows a range.
  PostgreSQL and MySQL document the same trap.

  Since results can't show which index was used, the rules can change later
  without touching any format. Only the index definition in the log binds us.

  Decision: s4ql picks the index by fixed rules, no statistics, with the exact
  rules being mine to settle from the note. Agree?

✻ Worked for 1m 17s · done Friday 1:42 PM

❯ And I guess we need to start doing benchmarks and other performance           
  monitoring that we can watch over time.                                       

⏺ Yes, and indexes are the right moment, since they are the first feature whose
  whole point is speed. My proposal is two kinds of measurement, because they
  fail in different ways:

  - Counts, which are exact. Rows read from the cache per query, requests and
    bytes to S3 per commit, allocations. These don't vary by machine, so tests
    can pin them and fail on a regression, in line with our rule that tests
    never depend on timing. For indexes, "rows read" is the number that shows
    the 400-times difference the research measured.
  - Timings, which are noisy. Ordinary Go benchmarks of statements, commits, and
    replay, each run next to the same work on plain SQLite on the same machine.
    The ratio to SQLite is far steadier than the raw time, also on CI's shared
    machines. CI records the results on every merge to main, and a chart shows
    them over time.

  I'd start with the counts, as part of the index slice, and add the timings and
  the chart as a piece of work of their own right after.

  Does that split match what you have in mind?

✻ Churned for 3s · done Friday 2:08 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Branch `update-delete-engine`, PR #91
  (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/91), reviewer markuswustenberg, not
  merged. Main is merged in (after #88/#86/#89; one conflict in
  internal/core/tx_test.go, where I kept both sides). After the merge, `make
  test`, `make lint`, `make check-browser`, and the engine's
  TestDifferential/TestSimulation/TestConn_differential without -short all pass.
  I ran no asan: neither the binding nor a fuzz test changed.

  **How rows change (point 3):** an update or delete finds its rows exactly as a
  select does (`scan`, `keyAccess`, `filter`, and `scanAll` covers it). While
  the scan runs, it adds each found key to a set in a temp table of the cache;
  only that set changes, never the scanned table. Then `KeySet.All` hands back
  one key at a time, each read by its own statement (`where (k...) > (last)
  order by ... limit 1`) that has finished before the row changes (`changeRows`
  in internal/engine/operator.go). So no cursor is ever open on a table while it
  changes, which is the case SQLite leaves undefined. A row the statement
  writes, even under a new key, is never in the set, so nothing is changed twice
  or missed, including when SET writes the key or the WHERE columns. No rows
  are held in memory. Rows change in key order, which is also SQLite's order for
  a multi-row update that changes the key. A key change is delete plus
  `insertNew`; a key equal by SQLite's comparison (-0.0 for 0.0) is written in
  place with `upsert`.

  **Core API added:** `Tx.Keys(ctx, name, func(*KeySet) error) error`,
  `KeySet.Add(ctx, key)`, `KeySet.All(ctx) iter.Seq2`. The set is dropped, with
  its at-rest statements finalized, when the call ends. I also moved the
  temp-table creation out of `touch` into `createKeyTable`. Engine `writer`
  gained `delete` and `keys`.

  **Error order (checked against SQLite):** no such table; SET columns in text
  order (`no such column: v`, the value, never the quotes question); WHERE
  columns by slice 7's rule; too-big SET values, then WHERE values; WHERE type
  refusal (as for a select); read-only error; NOT NULL for SET columns in table
  order, key included, only if a row is found; SET type refusal in table order;
  then per row in key order, record too big, then UNIQUE.

  **Decisions recorded in docs/todo.md ("Decisions of the engine step of slice
  8"):**
  - The rows are changed through the key set, for the reason above.
  - Rows change in key order.
  - The error order above.
  - SET's exact-type rule is static: it refuses even when no row is found, and
  comes after NOT NULL and the read-only error.
  - A SET literal over the length limit is too big even when no row is found,
  the same known difference as for WHERE literals.
  - A key change is delete plus insert; an equal key is written in place.
  - The count: every found row, including unchanged ones. 0 after a failure at
  step time (NOT NULL, UNIQUE, record too big, read-only); left as it was after
  prepare/bind errors or "not supported". `LastInsertId` stays 0.
  - The commit format is unchanged. A statement that changes no row leaves no
  commit object, so its annotation is not in the log (existing core behaviour).
  - Telemetry: kinds `update` and `delete` only.
  - Wrappers removed, with new rules `stmttest.UpdateRule`, `DeleteRule`,
  `WhereRule`, `FoundQuery`; the `excusedType` pins moved from 478 to 483
  (TestDifferential) and from 231 to 234 (drivercheck), from the update in
  `stmttest.Others`.

  **For the next builder (generated checks), also in the todo:**
  - Turn on `Generator.Changes` or the hooks in `stmttest.Sequence`.
  - `pair.bothWays` reruns only selects; running an update both ways needs a
  second engine, or `engine.AccessOf` extended to cover update and delete.
  - `checkLength` and `longCondition` know only insert and select literals.
  - `reach` and the tallies count no update or delete yet.
  - drivercheck compares the count through the pool only for inserts.
  - The simulation needs a planted bug in `changeRows` or `Tx.Keys`.
  - Mutation testing of the slice 8 code comes after that.

  **Please have the independent reviewer look at:**
  - `update.check`, which reads the first found row for NOT NULL; this is why
  sink `check` now takes ctx.
  - `dropKeys` after a failed callback or a savepoint rollback.
  - `UpdateRule`'s lazy found query.
  - `attempt.unchanged` in simulation_test.go, which excuses acknowledged 0-row
  updates and deletes from the in-log check. My code-review pass found it and I
  fixed it; the writer sub-agent found the `tableOf` panic at 300 seeds and the
  read-only count exception, both fixed.
  - Possible merge friction in internal/sql/mutation/equivalent.txt: I renamed
  the `Tx.touch` entries to `Tx.createKeyTable` and `#3` to `#2`.

  **Permission refusals:** Bash heredocs or Python scripts containing backticks,
  and one loop that ran git inside a pipeline, were refused as "too complex to
  verify that it stays inside the worktree". I used the Edit tool and plain
  commands instead. The writer sub-agent said it read elements-of-style.md from
  the fabrik skill directory, outside the worktree.

⏺ The engine and driver of slice 8 are built (#91): update and delete now run
  end to end, compared with SQLite statement by statement.

  How rows get changed safely: the statement first collects the keys of the rows
  it found into a temp table in the cache, then changes them one key at a time.
  So no cursor is open on a table while it changes, which SQLite leaves
  undefined, and nothing is held in memory. A changed primary key is a delete
  plus an insert.

  Two agents started from it:

  - A Fable reviewer reads #91 independently, with most weight on what reaches
    the log and the cache. #91 merges only after that.
  - The builder of the generated checks adds updates and deletes to the
    generated comparisons with SQLite, the simulation with a planted bug, the
    driver check, and fuzz targets.

  Also still running: the mutation testing of slice 7 and the statement pass for
  indexes.

⏺ Agent "Mutation testing of slice 7" is waiting on its own background work · 1h
  7m 38s

› Message from fabrik:builder
  I built the statement pass for indexes (slice 9) on branch `index-stmt`,
  rebased on the newest origin/main and pushed: PR #92
  (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/92), reviewer markuswustenberg, not
  merged. `make test`, `make lint` and `make check-browser` pass, and so do the
  pass's tests and the engine's `TestDifferential`/`TestSimulation` without
  `-short`. I did not run `make test-asan`: no fuzz target and nothing in the
  binding changed.

  **Shapes**
  - `stmt.CreateIndex{Span, Index Name, Table Name, Columns []IndexColumn}`,
  with 1 to 2,000 columns and none twice.
  - `stmt.IndexColumn{Name Name, Descending bool}`: DESC sets it, ASC and
  nothing leave it false.
  - `stmt.DropIndex{Span, Index Name}`. `Parameters()` is 0 for both.

  **Generator hooks**
  - `Generator.Indexes int`: one statement in Indexes is a create index or a
  drop index, drawn after `Changes`.
  - `Generator.CreateIndex func(index, table stmt.Name) []stmt.IndexColumn`
  gives the columns.
  - New positions `IndexName`, `IndexTable`, `IndexColumn`, `DropIndex`; new
  kinds `KindCreateIndex`, `KindDropIndex`.
  - With the option off and the hook nil it draws exactly as before: a
  54,900-line dump of `Generator` and `Sequence` matched origin/main byte for
  byte. The pass's tests set `Indexes = 4`.

  **Decisions (in docs/todo.md, for you to confirm)**
  - A column named twice is not supported (`column named twice`).
  - An index with the name of a table, its own table included, is supported; the
  engine gives the error.
  - `'a'` in the column list is `single-quoted name`, as everywhere.
  - `((a))` is `parentheses`; `t.a` is `column with the name of a table`;
  TRUE/FALSE is `TRUE or FALSE`; COLLATE is `COLLATE`; anything else is `index
  on an expression`.
  - NULLS FIRST/LAST is `NULLS FIRST or NULLS LAST`; a 2,001st column is `index
  beyond SQLite's limit`.
  - An index name starting `sqlite_` or `s4ql_` is `reserved index name` in both
  statements; eponymous virtual table names are allowed as index names.
  - UNIQUE is `UNIQUE INDEX`, a WHERE is `partial index`.
  - `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN`, `INDEXED BY` and `NOT INDEXED` now each have their own
  name (they were `EXPLAIN` and `INDEXED BY or NOT INDEXED`). This changes the
  answer for texts the pass already refused.

  **What SQLite does in the point 2 cases**
  - Column twice: it makes an index with the column twice.
  - `'a'` is read as the name `a` (`'zz'` gives `no such column: zz`). `"a"` is
  a name, since the build has DQS off.
  - Keywords: 88 work as the index name, 89 as the table after ON, 84 as a
  column, 88 in DROP INDEX.
  - `main.i` works; `temp.i` gives `cannot create a TEMP index on non-TEMP
  table`; `on main.t` is `near ".": syntax error`.
  - An empty list is `near ")": syntax error`.
  - DESC is kept; NULLS gives `unsupported use of NULLS FIRST` on any database.
  - More than 2,000 columns gives `too many columns in index`, once the table is
  found.
  - `drop index main.i` works; `drop index temp.i` gives `no such index:
  temp.i`.

  **Schema-dependent errors for the engine builder, in SQLite's order**
  - CREATE INDEX:
    1. `no such table: main.<table>`
    2. `there is already a table named <index>`
    3. `index <index> already exists`
    4. Then each column in text order: `no such column: z`, or `no such column:
  "z" - should this be a string literal in single-quotes?` for a double-quoted
  name.
  - DROP INDEX: `no such index: <index>`, also when a table has that name.
  - All have code 1. I measured with the binding, but the tests don't check,
  that `no such column` has the name's offset and the others -1.
  - Also measured: `drop table` drops the table's indexes, a rollback undoes
  create and drop index, and SQLite's index key is the index columns followed by
  the primary key columns not already in it.

  **What I touched outside the pass**
  - New `internal/engine/index.go` with `indexRefusal`, called once at the end
  of `engine.check`. It gives `CREATE INDEX` at the CREATE and `DROP INDEX` at
  the INDEX, the errors the pass gave before; `TestCheck_indexes` pins both.
  - Comment-only edits in `conn.go` and the two `checkText` wrappers. No other
  engine or driver code changed.

  **Watch out when merging**
  - The `update-delete-engine` builder is removing the `checkText` wrappers and
  the driver's `engine.Check` call. Both must stay until the engine runs slice
  9, because the corpus the engine tests read has 1,302 supported CREATE/DROP
  INDEX texts. I said this in the slice 8 todo item.
  - Expect a small conflict in `engine.check`.
  - The roadmap/site status updates after the merge are left for your sub-agent.

  **What moved, and the review**
  - The pass's pinned numbers moved: mutants went from 64 to 39 syntax and from
  196 to 153 not supported where SQLite does not say, and near misses from 2,054
  to 1,531. I grouped every excused text by message: all are CREATE TABLE, none
  is an index.
  - The code review found no bugs and four small issues, all fixed: stale
  comments, the README not yet covering slice 9 (a writer sub-agent updated it),
  and a redundant condition in the 2,001st-column check.

  **Permission refusals** (I didn't work around any): Bash heredocs with
  backticks and a `for` loop of `git show` were refused as too complex to
  verify. I used scripts written to the scratchpad and one `git show` per
  command instead.

  The diary is `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a73b6010
  4f323fb9c/docs/diary/2026-11-20-index-stmt.md`, two steps.

✻ Cooked for 1m 6s · done Friday 2:24 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  Review of PR #91 (`origin/update-delete-engine`, 655bca7): I found no defect
  in what reaches the log or the cache. Two findings, neither in the product's
  write path, plus one test gap.

  **1. Medium, test only: `attempt.unchanged` excuses on the engine's own
  word.** `run.statement` and `run.verify` in
  `internal/engine/simulation_test.go`.
  - `unchanged` is set from `res.Changes == 0`, the engine's own count. `verify`
  then skips every succeeded write in the comparison with SQLite (`a.state !=
  succeeded || !a.query || a.write` → `continue`) and excuses `unchanged` from
  "is not in the log".
  - Scenario: a statement of its own, `delete from t where k = 5` or `update t
  set v = 1`, is acknowledged with 0 changes although rows match (a bug in
  `changeRows`, `keyAccess` or `Tx.Keys`, or a stale state). It leaves no commit
  object and no violation. Nothing runs it in SQLite at its position, and
  nothing checks that its position holds the writes acknowledged before it. The
  same holds for a transaction whose writes are all `unchanged`.
  - Confirmed by running: I planted `return 0, nil` at the top of `changeRows`
  and ran 300 seeds of the three workloads. Only 2 of 900 runs failed, both
  through an update inside a transaction whose commit held other writes. With a
  check added, seed 53 (default) showed an acknowledged `update range set x1$ =
  4` at commit 8, where SQLite changes 7 rows, with no violation of its own.
  - Reach today is small, because the only update the simulation draws is the
  one of `stmttest.Others`. It matters for the next step in `docs/todo.md`: a
  planted "forgets the last key" bug on a one-row update would be excused.
  - Fix: decide by SQLite. Run each acknowledged write that is not in the log in
  the oracle at `index[a.position]`, require no error and `changes() == 0`, and
  give it the "saw every write acknowledged before it started" check that
  selects get.

  **2. Low, undocumented difference: a multi-row update with a later row over
  the length limit.** `update.run` in `internal/engine/operator.go`; the claim
  is in `docs/todo.md` ("for each row … `string or blob too big`").
  - Scenario, limit 1000: `t (k int primary key, a text, b text not null)` with
  rows `(1,'','')` and `(2, <301 x>, '')`, then `update t set b = <700 x>`.
  SQLite 3.54.1 succeeds and stores a 1002-byte row; s4ql gives `string or blob
  too big`. Where the new key also collides, SQLite gives UNIQUE and s4ql gives
  too big.
  - Cause: SQLite's `OP_MakeRecord` checks the limit only when the output
  register's buffer from an earlier row is too small, and on macOS the buffer
  has the allocator's usable size.
  - Confirmed by running on macOS: 15 mismatches in 40 random seeds, all of this
  shape; a one-row update agrees. From SQLite's source, not run: on Linux the
  slack is at most about 7 bytes.
  - s4ql's answer is the deterministic one, and it needs a record over the limit
  (1 GB by default), so the code should stay. Fix: record it as a known
  difference in `docs/todo.md`, and have the generated checks excuse it;
  otherwise they will fail on a Mac and pass in CI.

  **3. Test gap, behaviour correct: no test cancels an update or a delete
  midway.** I ran one with a context that flips after n `Err()` calls, for n = 0
  to 200 over 40 rows, as a statement of its own and in a transaction. The
  state was unchanged every time, the transaction went on and committed, and the
  replay was equal.

  **What I checked and found right**
  - **Random differential against SQLite, by running:** 40 seeds of 150
  statements each on their own (by key and scan-all), and 40 seeds of
  multi-statement transactions with rollbacks and drop/create inside. They
  covered int, real, text and blob keys (-0.0/0.0, `''`, `'a'`/`'A'`, `é` in
  both normal forms, `x''`/`x'00'`/`x'0000'`, the int64 edges), key changes onto
  free, own and taken keys, WHERE on the columns SET writes, and NULL/NOT NULL.
  Errors, `changes()` after every statement including failures, rows inside the
  transaction, state, and state after replay into an empty cache were all
  equal.
  - **`Tx.Keys`, `KeySet`, `dropKeys`, by reading:**
    - The `leave` calls balance.
    - The set is dropped after a failed callback.
    - A panic or a failed transaction leaves the table to the rollback of the
  savepoint or the transaction, so none survives a commit.
    - `tx.temps` only grows, so names never collide after `RollbackTo`.
    - Statements are finalized or forgotten.
    - The `>` iteration compares as the table's own key does; `bind` folds -0.0
  and text is BINARY.
  - **Driver and probes, by running:** NUL or invalid UTF-8 text and NaN/Inf
  arguments are refused before anything changes. A column set twice and reserved
  or temp table names are refused by the pass. A point update in a transaction
  takes 74µs against 17µs for an insert, and a whole-table update 7µs per row.
  - **The PR's own tests:** the new tests passed under `-race` (`TestTx_Keys`
  ×5, the engine's ×3), and `TestUpdateDelete` passed. They compare with real
  SQLite where they say they do.

  Ready to merge as it is: yes, once the `browser` check passes — it was still
  in progress when I looked, and the other 15 had passed. Findings 1 and 2
  belong in the brief of the builder of the generated checks. My worktree is
  clean: nothing committed, pushed or commented.

  - A blind spot in the simulation. A statement that reports zero changed rows
    was excused on the engine's own word. The reviewer planted a bug that makes
    every update and delete do nothing, and only 2 of 900 runs caught it. The
    fix is to ask SQLite whether zero is right.

    whether SQLite refuses a too-long row depends on the platform's memory
    allocator: it passes on macOS and fails on Linux. s4ql refuses it
    everywhere, which stays, and the difference gets recorded and excused
    precisely.

⏺ Agent "Mutation testing of slice 7" finished · 2h 16m 32s

❯ Idea for later: allow reads from instance without lease if commit id given?   

⏺ Agent(Todo: reads without lease idea) Sonnet 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Good idea, and it holds up: a read at a named commit reads a fixed point in
  the log, so any instance can serve it correctly once its cache has caught up
  to that commit, lease or not. A caller that passes the commit of its own last
  write sees its own writes, and reads can spread over instances. It's going
  into the todo as an idea for later, with the open points: "exactly that
  commit" or "that commit or later", and how a commit id travels through
  database/sql.

  Meanwhile, the mutation testing of slice 7 is done (#93): 1,222 mutants in the
  where code, none left unexplained, and no fault found in the code. It added
  tests that kill 26 survivors and merged the duplicated type check into one
  function. A small agent is merging it.

✻ Cooked for 33s · done Friday 3:26 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  Commit 20ac604, pushed to main. It adds one item at the end of the section
  "The storage core" in docs/todo.md. Only that file changed.
  Spec today: in "Concurrency", "Always a lease" says an instance must hold the
  lease to read or write and there is no mode without it, and "An instance
  without a valid lease refuses and keeps trying" says its reads fail at once
  with a temporary error.

⏺ The idea is in the todo (20ac604). For when we pick it up: the spec today says
  the opposite, under "Always a lease": an instance must hold the lease to read
  or write, and its reads fail at once without it. So this would be a change to
  an agreed rule, not only an addition.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  **Branch `index-core`, PR #97 — work complete, but the PR is CONFLICTING and
  CI has not run.** I did not merge `origin/main`: #92 (`index-stmt`) is still
  open, and the conflicts are #92 vs #91 in `conn.go`,
